- 2026-10-21: **Stress-accent audit** (the 12 stress-accent errors the latest tuning bench left unasked in single layer lines, all of them in `work/probes/unasked-set`, each crop looked at; the largest unasked class, 42 over tuning and validation). **Six are ours**: the print sets an emphasis accent that every reader and the layer drop ("zíjn" twice, "Ík", "níét", "míjn", "vríénden"; every one on an i). **Six are the reference's**: our text matches the print and the publisher's text departs from it, five with an accent the print has and the reference lacks ("eén", "làat", "Orònzo", "Christóph", and one more), one with an accent the reference has and the print lacks ("vóór"; "Oké" the same). So about half the class is the measure's, not the build's, and a verdict batch would settle those (human verdicts only: not written from a look at a crop). The real half is one pattern: an acute accent on an i for emphasis, read as plain. Whether a reader prompt can keep it is the open question; the five read prompts tried earlier were judged on the whole set, not on this pattern.
- 2026-10-21: **Emphasis accents: a prompt can't keep them, a higher resolution keeps two of six** (`experiments/probe_accent_prompt.py`, `probe_accent_dpi.py`; the 12 audited lines, the six real errors for the DPI test). A sentence added to the book's read prompt ("keep every accent mark as printed, including an acute accent on a single letter for emphasis, add none"): the reading holds the same words as without it on all 12 (the reference's word on 1, the build's on 7 both ways); the accents it should add, it doesn't. Crops at 450 and 600 DPI instead of 300: the reference's word on 2 of the 6 real errors ("níét", "vríénden") against 0, the other four lost at every resolution. The reader set's earlier DPI test found a higher resolution fixes as many lines as it breaks, so two of six is no reason to change the crop; the loss of an emphasis accent on an i is the readers', all of them, and the next step would be a reader that sees the accent, not a better prompt for one that doesn't.
- 2026-10-21: **No reader keeps the emphasis accents at 300 DPI** (`experiments/probe_accent_readers.py`; the six real errors of the audit): glm-ocr holds the reference's word on 0 of 6, olmOCR on 1 ("níét"), the read model 0 (2 at 450 DPI); every reader writes the plain letter. On the six lines where the print and the build agree and the reference departs, olmOCR sides with the reference on 4 and glm-ocr on 3: it adds or drops accents the print doesn't have, so its agreement with a reference is no proof of the print. The emphasis accent on an i is below what the line crops carry, for every model tried; the answer is a reader that sees the accent (a crop around the word, at a higher resolution, only where a word is a likely target), which a hand rule can't pick (checklist: stress accents).

- 2026-10-21: **A per-book alphabet from the scan (the user's idea), assessed and its first test** (`experiments/probe_glyph_segmentation.py`, no models). The target is large: 203 of the 527 errors left unasked over tuning and validation are glyph-identity errors (letters 81, stress accents 46, case 43, other accents and diaereses 23, quote glyphs 10), about a quarter of them the reference's slips. Its weak points: the errors aren't uncertain readings (every reader writes the same plain letter), so fitting only unsure characters never fires; an alphabet built from the readers' confident readings inherits their systematic errors, so glyph shapes must be clustered before they are labelled; most accent errors are singletons (the six real ones audited sit in six different places) and don't make a cluster; the text layer's character boxes are fake (every character of "zijn" is 3.6 pt wide: the word box cut evenly) and Internet Archive's per-letter data is public only for public-domain items. **First test, can a line crop be cut into one blob per glyph?** On the 340 control lines of the frozen reader set (known text, edge fragments and specks dropped): blobs equal the expected count on 34% of lines, within one on 59%; touching letters (rn, ri, cl) merge on 28% of lines, extra marks and broken letters add on 18%. So a plain split can't align a whole line, but a third of lines give clean, correctly labelled glyphs: thousands a book, enough for templates of the common letters. Next, if it is pursued: whether the dot of an i can be told from an acute mark by its shape (size, elongation, slant) against the book's own i-dots, on the six real accent cases and the clean lines (an hour, no models); then whether odd glyph clusters (the dotless ı) form without labels. Kill if neither separates.
- 2026-10-21: **How often a book prints a rare accented glyph** (the references of 34 books, counts only: í Í ì ó Ó ò ú ù á à and the dotless ı): 24 of 34 books print one of them at least 5 times, 16 at least 20 times; Het schaduwhuis prints "í" 82 times, Het heft in eigen hand 59, Goede dochter "ó" 69. So a per-book alphabet does see dozens of instances of the glyph in the books that use it (this weakens the singleton objection: each book is its own font, and within a book the glyph repeats). The same count caps the prize: about 1,800 such glyphs in all against 46 unasked stress-accent errors, half of them the reference's slips, so the readers already get about 98.7% of rare accents right; the accent part of the idea is worth at most about 4% of the unasked errors (23 of 527). The larger part of the glyph-identity class is letters (81) and case (43).

What is odd over a stem in a whole book is mostly other print: the second dot of a colon, a semicolon, a quote mark, the diaeresis and accents the language uses, a j's dot. A threshold on one oddness number can't tell them apart. To go on it would need the mark classified (what it stands over, and its shape against that letter's own marks) with a learned model on mark-level labels, which the references only give per line. - 2026-11-03: **A short word leaning a little among upright ones is no italic: BETTER on tuning and validation** (the user's spot-check of Hoe wij verdwijnen: "het *op* te geven", "*een* van het bejaardentehuis"; `italics._italic`, `_upright`, warm, slants measured again). Lone words, by slant from the page's upright, heap at 8–9° on every book measured, dip at 10° and rise from 11°; by eye, Hoe wij verdwijnen's lone upright words ("op", "een", "en", "goed", "geen", "zeggen") lean 9–11°, Artemis' ("good", "of", "faced") 8–9° and its real lone emphasis ("*quite*", "*some*", "*anyone*", "*are*", "*not*") 8–11°. What separates them is the stems measured: one or two against three or more. A lone word on fewer than three stems needs 12°; a run's neighbours reach over line ends. Tuning against `tuning-20261103T052649`: false italics 433 → 331, missed 2,022 → 1,995 (italic errors 4,206 → 4,075 with Het schaduwhuis), words, breaks and headings unchanged; validation: false 245 → 162, missed 166 → 166 (411 → 328). - 2026-11-07: **The OCR judges' calibration, by confidence bucket** (no models; the trust data's 28,342 suspects with a right version over every book, each judge's pick against the reference), prompted by a vendor post on cascading LLM judges (a cheap decision model on everything, a strong model only inside a confidence band; what decides whether that works is calibration, a cheap model wrong at high confidence can't be cascaded; the largest gain for every model came from naming concrete error kinds in the prompt). clef:27b is under-confident and well ordered: below 0.5 right 74% (31% of suspects), 0.5–0.7 94% (28%), 0.7–0.9 98.4% (39%), 0.9+ 99% (2%). Winnow-12B is roughly calibrated: 57%, 75%, 88% and 97% in the same buckets (28%, 20%, 33%, 18%). Neither is wrong when sure, so either could stand early in a cascade; the arbiter already learns how far to trust each, so a raw-confidence cascade would repeat it. - 2026-11-11: **Retraining the arbiter is exact, and 72 changed suspects in 30,647 move it by 15 unasked errors** (no bench needed: `work/models/ocr-trust-rep.pkl`, trained with today's code on production's trust data of 2026-11-07, is byte for byte production's arbiter, 23 min). The control's trust data (the same books rebuilt on the small-capitals code, the doubts off) differs from production's in the versions of 72 suspects, the capitals added to mixed-case words, and in nothing else: same rows, same votes, same labels. The arbiter trained on it left 539 errors unasked on tuning against 524, with 36 fewer asked and three more heading errors. So the trees turn on a few dozen rows, and a difference of some fifteen unasked errors between two retrainings is inside what a retraining does by itself, not evidence for the change that came with it. That reads back on pass 4 (551 → 535 on tuning, 170 → 178 on validation, not taken) and forward on batch five: judge a retrained arbiter against a second one retrained on the same data with a few rows changed, or on the trust data's leave-one-book-out scores, not on one bench. The arbiter's variance is the thing to bring down (more trees averaged, or seeds bagged) before the next retraining is weighed.

- 2026-11-11: **The bench counts an italic word by its place, whatever its letters**
  (`evaluate.score`: the words of a differing stretch of equal length are paired in
  order). A misread word set italic where the reference is italic counted as an italic
  error, so the count rose with the questions asked and decided the verdict three times
  today. A glued or split italic word has no place of its own and still counts. Runs
  before this change count italics the old way: compare italic errors only between
  runs made after it.

  model agrees most with glm-ocr's reading at 300 dpi: 759, a best setting picked for

  (lines gained over 300 dpi against agreement gained, 32 book-settings: r = +0.72;

  signal is real, but the prize is 7 lines in 800 over one fixed setting, on a set

  differs from the others' by one to three lines of fifty: noise. Nothing failed on a
  model here; there is no room. The pipeline's readers at 450 dpi were not taken as
  a swap on 2026-10-22, which stands. The other variants (contrast, crop padding,
  prompt wording) were not read. The checklist's per-book settings step is dropped.
- 2026-11-11: **A book's typeface can be found from its scan: eight of the user's ten
  books named, the first step of matched-font rendering** (the user's idea of render
  and compare; `experiments/probe_font_match.py`, no models, about two minutes a book).
  240 plain lower-case words of five letters or more are cut from twelve pages of the
  scan, each drawn in every font of the user's library (2,317 with Latin letters: Adobe
  Font Folio 11 and a folder with Quadraat, Scala and Swift, outside the repo) and laid
  over the scan's word at its height; a font's score is the mean correlation, at the
  letter spacing (0.92–1.08) and stroke thickening (−3 to +5 scan pixels) that suit it.
  Best and next family: Stella Quadraat 0.66 (Life 0.61); Nervous System Janson Text
  0.75 (Galliard 0.74); Andere levens Janson Text 0.83 (Garamond Premier 0.76); Een
  Russische roman Janson Text 0.85 (Adobe Caslon 0.75); Hoe wij verdwijnen Janson Text
  0.82 (Garamond Premier 0.74); De kus in de nacht Trump Mediaeval 0.76 (Quadraat 0.63);
  Pieter Daens Minion condensed 0.76 (weights within 0.01 of each other); The Loudest
  Sound and Nothing Sabon 0.76 (Minister 0.71). Undecided: Ex-minnaar (Galliard 0.692,
  Bembo 0.685) and Going into a Dark House (Bembo 0.663, Fournier 0.662, Dante 0.656),
  the two blurriest scans. Learned on the way: the layer's word boxes cut through
  letters, so a word must be cut along its ink (the first run, on the boxes, ranked
  bold sans-serifs first); a scan's ink is heavier than a clean drawing, so a bold cut
  scores level with its regular: the family is found, the weight not yet. The right
  font scores 0.66–0.85, enough to name a family, not to settle a contested word:
  that needs the drawing fitted to the book's ink on its own letters (own-type
  templates). By eye beforehand the agent had guessed Scala for Stella: wrong.
- 2026-11-11: **Render and compare, the user's idea: a judge that knows no language.
  First test on Stella: the better fit is the print on 9 of 9 labelled places, where
  each judge has 5 or 6** (`experiments/probe_render_compare.py`, no models, seconds a
  book once its font is matched). The idea (the user, 2026-11-11): every reader and
  judge is a language model and they fail together, reading what makes sense; most of
  their mistakes are plain to a person looking at the ink. So take a contested place,
  draw each version as the book would print it, lay the drawings over the scan, and let
  the better fit vote, "as if you were to ask someone who can't read to compare two
  pictures". It should help with specks, small capitals, ligatures, accents; not with
  line-end hyphens, paragraph breaks, washed-out pages or anything the ink doesn't
  show. Its parts, by the names agreed: **matched-font rendering** (the user's: the
  drawing in the book's typeface, found from the scan; the entry above), **own-type
  templates** (the drawing from letters cut out of the book's own uncontested words:
  the real ink, but no rare shapes, as the user pointed out: a lone í, "VTM" in small
  capitals, which is where the readers struggle most, so the font is the main road
  and the book's letters calibrate it), **leftover ink** (the agreed reading drawn and
  taken from the scan: what is left raises a suspect where every reading agrees, the
  85% of errors no question asks about; not built). How the test works: a suspect's
  box is its span's box in the layer; the span's ink is cut along the blank around it
  (`probe_font_match.word_ink`), each version is drawn whole in the book's regular cut
  (Stella: Quadraat) with the font's fitted stroke thickening, scaled to the ink's
  height and laid over it at the best of fifteen stretches (0.86–1.14); the fit is the
  correlation of the two, blurred a little. Stella's nine settled places (labelled by
  the user's answers), fit of each version, the print first: "íéts" 0.88 against
  "iéts" 0.86 and "féts" 0.73; "ís" 0.91, "is" 0.85; "gestuit" 0.91, "gestuït" 0.88;
  "grof werk" 0.84, "grofwerk" 0.77; "trof ik" 0.83, "trofik" 0.64; "wat er" 0.90,
  "water" 0.53; "besef een" 0.91, "besefeen" 0.66; "omging." 0.93, "omgeving." 0.58;
  "Ze" 0.92, "ze" 0.51. Every place a judge missed, the fit has right (clef 4 misses,
  the others 3 each), among them both stress accents the user corrected by hand and
  both glued words that were Stella's unasked errors. Accent margins are thin (0.02 to
  0.06), spaces and letters wide (0.07 to 0.4). Nine places in one book whose typeface
  the library holds exactly: encouraging, not proof. Next: every golden book's
  typeface matched (running), then the same test on their ~30,000 labelled places by
  kind of error and by where the judges were wrong; if it holds, the fit as a judge's
  vote with its margin in the trust data, the arbiter retrained, both benches.
